Module 5 — Becoming an Engine · Lesson 5.7

An ECS from Scratch: Storage Design

Lesson 5.6 measured what a scene layout costs and came out with three constraints an ECS has to satisfy — dense and compactable, systems that declare what they touch, no virtual dispatch on the hot path. It then refused to choose between the two designs that satisfy all three, because nothing it measured distinguishes them. Both are dense. Both are contiguous. Both let a system touch a subset. This lesson takes the measurement that does distinguish them — and it takes it before either is built, because the two designs differ in exactly two operations and both of those can be simulated in three hundred lines. Then it chooses, argues the choice from the numbers, and states plainly the scene at which the choice becomes the wrong one.

Time
≈ 5 hours
Prereqs
5.4 — Handles: Generational Indices, 5.6 — Data-Oriented Design, 3.10 — Profiling the Rasterizer
Files
scratch/ecs_probe.hpp, scratch/bench_57.cpp, scratch/measure_57.py, scratch/verify_57.cpp
Milestone
A decided architecture, with the evidence attached: five tables, a stated cost model for each design, a recommendation, and the one sentence that says when to revisit it. No engine code changes — 5.8 writes the container, and it will write the one this lesson chose.

By the end of this lesson you will be able to…

  • …describe an archetype and a sparse set precisely enough to draw each one's memory, and name the single structural difference between them.
  • …explain why a sparse-set lookup is a two-deep dependency chain and why that costs 2.4× rather than 6.5× — from Lesson 5.6's memory-level parallelism, not from folklore.
  • …simulate a design you have not written, well enough to time it, and say what such a simulation can and cannot tell you.
  • …state what a structural change costs in each design, and why one of the two answers contains the entity's width and the other does not.
  • …separate a codegen effect from a memory effect in your own results, using one rebuild.
  • …quantify the sparse set's memory overhead and say which term in it grows with the number of component types.
  • …explain what a group is, why it makes the migration one-directional, and why that asymmetry — rather than any single ratio — is what decides this.
  • …defend the choice this course makes, and name the scene shape at which you would make the other one.

1The Problem

Lesson 5.6 ended by handing this lesson three constraints, each with a number attached: storage must be dense and compactable (a decayed pointer tree cost 6.47× a flat array at 100,000 objects); a system must declare which components it touches (a loop reading 12 of 96 bytes ran 5× faster when the other 84 were stored elsewhere, and a loop reading 60 of 96 gained nothing); and iteration must not be virtual (a flat 1.5–1.7× at every size, including four objects sitting in L1).

Those constraints are real and they were earned. They also do not decide anything, and 5.6 said so on its last page. The two storage designs that every production ECS is built out of both satisfy all three:

The two candidate ECS storage designs and the engines that use each.
designthe idea in one sentenceused by
Archetype Entities with the same component set are stored together, so the components a query wants are parallel dense arrays walked by one shared index. Unity DOTS, Flecs, Unreal Mass
Sparse set Each component type gets one dense array, plus a sparse map from entity to position in it; a query walks one pool and looks the rest up. EnTT

Both are dense. Both are compactable — each keeps its live data packed and moves the last element into any hole. Both let a system name the components it touches and fetch nothing else. Neither uses a virtual call to iterate. Every number 5.6 published applies equally to both, which is exactly why 5.6 was told not to let this lesson inherit them.

The trap this lesson is built to avoid

There is a third way to decide, and it is the one that would be easiest here: we already own something shaped like a sparse set. Lesson 5.4's pool<T> has exactly three arrays — slots_ sparse and stable, items_ dense and packed, owners_ the way back — which is a sparse set with generations bolted on. Adopting sparse sets because that is what is lying around is choosing an architecture by what is convenient, and §3.4 explains why the resemblance is a hint and not an argument.

So this lesson does what 5.6 did: it measures. The unusual part is when. Normally you cannot compare two architectures without building both, which costs weeks and produces a sunk cost that then argues for itself. Here you can, because the two designs differ in exactly two operations — reading K components of every matching entity, and adding or removing one component from one entity — and both of those are access patterns that fit in a few hundred lines with no entity manager, no component registry, no type erasure and no scheduler around them. Everything else about the two designs is orthogonal to the question.

Five measurements follow, and one of them changed the recommendation from "sparse set, reluctantly, because it is less code" to "sparse set, confidently, because the migration only runs one way". No engine source changes this lesson; the output is a decision, and 5.8 builds it.

2Building Intuition

2.1What a query actually is

Strip away the vocabulary and an ECS does one thing over and over: for every entity that has all of these components, run this code on them. That is a query, and the engine's existing render loop already is one — it walks four scene_objects and, for each, reads a transform and a mesh handle. Written as a query it would say: for every entity with a transform and a mesh, build a model matrix and submit a draw.

Two numbers describe any query and they are the two axes of everything below. K is how many component types it names — one for "advance every particle's age", four for a render query that wants a transform, a mesh, a material and a bounding volume. The selectivity is what fraction of the world matches: a transform query matches everything, a "has a rigid body and is asleep" query matches almost nothing.

The reason those two numbers matter is that they are precisely where the two designs pull apart. Reaching K components is cheap for an archetype and costs a lookup per extra component for a sparse set. Matching a small fraction is cheap for an archetype, which never visits a non-matching entity, and awkward for a sparse set, whose dense arrays hold everything. So before any measurement: we should expect the archetype to win the query, and the interesting question is by how much, at what size, and what it charges for the privilege.

2.2The two answers, as memory

Draw both and the difference is one column wide.

The archetype's arrangement is a promise about identity: row i of the transform column and row i of the material column are the same entity, and that is true because the chunk was built to make it true. Every entity in the chunk has exactly the same component set. An entity with a transform but no material is not in this chunk; it is in another one, with its own columns. The consequence is that a query touching four components is four sequential streams and one loop counter — no lookups, no branches, no possibility of a miss that was not going to happen anyway.

The sparse set makes no such promise. Each component type owns one dense array holding every instance of that type in the world, in whatever order they were inserted. To find entity 17's velocity you read velocity.sparse[17], which gives an index into velocity.data. The dense array is still dense — 5.6's first constraint is satisfied — but its order is nobody's business, and in particular it is not guaranteed to be the same order as the transform pool's.

The sparse array is bigger than it looks

Note what sparse is indexed by: the entity id. So it has one slot for every entity that exists, not for every entity that has this component. A world with 100,000 entities of which 200 are lights still pays 400 KB for the light component's sparse array. §3.3 puts a number on that, and it is the sparse set's real structural cost — the one that is almost never quoted alongside the query ratios.

2.3The redirect, and why it is not a linked list

The instinct after Lesson 5.6 is alarm. sparse[e] and then data[idx] is a load whose address comes out of another load — the exact shape that made a linked list cost 6.47× while an array of pointers cost 2.38×. If a sparse-set query is a linked list, the argument is over before it starts.

It is not, and 5.6 already contains the reason. What made the linked list catastrophic was not the indirection; it was that the next address was inside the current node, so nothing could be issued until the previous miss returned and the latencies added up in series. An array of pointers, by contrast, has all its addresses available immediately, so a core can have a dozen dependent loads outstanding at once and the misses overlap. That is memory-level parallelism, and it is the whole difference between 2.4× and 6.5×.

A sparse-set query is the second picture, twice over. The loop reads xf.dense[i] sequentially, so entity ids arrive by the cache line — sixteen at a time — and each one starts an independent two-deep chain. Nothing in entity 17's chain has to wait for entity 4's. So the cost is bounded by how many chains the core can keep in flight, not by the number of entities times the memory latency.

That reframes the question usefully. The redirect is latency, not bandwidth, and latency is the kind of cost a machine is good at hiding — if there is something else for it to be doing. Which raises the question §4.4 exists to answer: how much work does the body of the query do? If a system reads four components and does thirty nanoseconds of arithmetic per entity, several extra loads may cost literally nothing. If it does two nanoseconds, they will not.

3The Theory

3.1Four operations, and the two that differ

An ECS runtime has to be good at four things. Writing them down first is what keeps the comparison from becoming an argument about which design is nicer.

The four operations an ECS runtime performs, with each design's asymptotic cost.
operationarchetypesparse setdiffers?
Create / destroy an entity append a row / swap-and-pop append to each pool / swap-and-pop each no — both O(components)
Get one component of one entity two lookups: which archetype, which row one lookup: sparse[e] marginally, and both are O(1)
Query K components walk K parallel arrays, one index walk one array, K−1 redirects yes — §5.1
Add / remove a component move the entity to another archetype push or swap-and-pop in one pool yes — §5.3

Two rows out of four are identical in shape, which is worth noticing on its own: most of what an ECS does, both designs do the same way. The entire architectural argument lives in the other two rows, and they point in opposite directions — the archetype is faster at the one that happens thousands of times per frame, and the sparse set is faster at the one that happens when something spawns, dies, gets picked up, catches fire or goes to sleep.

Which means the answer cannot be a ratio. It has to be a rate comparison: how many queries per frame against how many structural changes per frame, weighted by what each costs. That is the shape of the decision, and §6 does the arithmetic with the measured numbers.

3.2What a structural change costs, on paper

Predict before you measure — a wrong prediction is worth more than no prediction, and this one turns out to be wrong in an instructive way.

An entity gains a component. In the archetype design its component set just changed, so by definition it no longer belongs where it is: it must move from the chunk for {transform, velocity, bounds} to the chunk for {transform, velocity, bounds, material}. Everything it owns travels. Then the source chunk has a hole in it, which swap-and-pop fills by moving its last row down — so a second entity's data moves too, and that entity's row index has to be patched.

Worked example — the byte count

Our probe's entity carries a transform (60 B), a velocity (16 B) and a bounds (16 B), which is 92 bytes that must travel, plus a 16-byte material constructed at the destination.

Copy 92 B into the destination chunk. Move 92 B within the source chunk to close the hole. Write two index entries. Call it ≈ 200 bytes touched.

Now widen the entity to twelve components — eight more at 16 B each. The travelling payload becomes 92 + 128 = 220 B, and the total ≈ 456 bytes. So the byte model predicts the wide case costs 456 / 200 = 2.3× the narrow one.

Measured, at 100,000 entities: 13.10 ns narrow, 58.48 ns wide. That is 4.5×, twice what the model says.

The model is not merely imprecise, it is measuring the wrong thing, and the correction is worth having because it generalises. A column is a separate allocation. Eight extra components are eight more std::vectors, in eight unrelated places in the heap, and a move has to touch each of them twice — once to read the entity's row and once to move the chunk's last row into the hole. That is sixteen additional independent memory touches, most of them missing at this working-set size. 45.4 ns for sixteen touches is 2.8 ns each, which is about right for a partially-overlapped access to memory on this machine.

So the cost model is not "bytes moved", it is independent memory streams touched, and the count of those is two per column, per move. Write it down, because it is the sentence the decision rests on: an archetype's structural change costs a fixed price per component the entity has, whether or not that component has anything to do with the change.

The sparse set's version of the same operation is one paragraph shorter and that is the point. Push the component onto material.data, push the entity id onto material.dense, write material.sparse[e]. Three writes, two of them to the end of arrays that are already warm because the last insert touched them, and nothing else in the world is read or written. The entity's transform, velocity and bounds are not merely left alone; they are not known about. The cost has no term for how wide the entity is because the operation has no way to find out.

3.3What the index costs, on paper

The sparse set pays for that O(1) somewhere, and the bill arrives in memory. Per component type it keeps a dense array of entity ids (4 B per entity that has the component) and a sparse array of indices (4 B per entity in the world, present or not). The archetype keeps one entity id per row and nothing else.

Worked example — index overhead at two scales

Our probe: 100,000 entities, 4 component types, all present on all entities. Payload is 60 + 16 + 16 + 16 = 108 B each = 10.8 MB. Archetype adds 4 B per entity for the id: 400 KB. Sparse adds 4 B per type for the dense id and 4 B per type for the sparse slot: 8 × 4 × 100,000 = 3.2 MB. Overhead over the archetype: 25%.

A real game: 100,000 entities, 32 component types, of which an average entity has 6. The archetype still adds 400 KB. The sparse set's dense arrays scale with what is actually present — 6 × 4 × 100,000 = 2.4 MB — but its sparse arrays are indexed by entity id and so are sized by the world, not by the component: 32 × 4 × 100,000 = 12.8 MB, of which roughly 80% is the value meaning "this entity does not have this component".

Twelve megabytes of mostly-nothing is not a rounding error; it is three times this machine's L2. This is why EnTT pages its sparse arrays — allocating them in 4,096-entry blocks and only materialising a block when something in its range gets the component — which costs one more indirection on every lookup to save the memory.

Whether this course needs paging is a question with a pleasant answer, and it is another dividend from Lesson 5.4. A sparse array's size is set by the highest entity id ever issued, and 5.4's pool<T> issues indices from a free list, reusing the most recently freed slot first. So the id space stays compact: its high-water mark is the largest number of entities that were ever simultaneously alive, not the number ever created. At ten component types and a ten-thousand-entity high-water mark that is 400 KB of sparse array in total, and paging would be complexity spent on nothing.

…with one failure mode, and it is the same one 5.4 documented

High-water marks do not come back down. A level that spawns a million particles as entities and then destroys them leaves a million-slot sparse array per component type for the rest of the process — 4 MB each, whether or not anything is alive. 5.4 said the same thing about slots_, and the answer is the same: things that exist in millions and die in seconds should not be entities. A particle system owns its own array and is one entity.

3.4The one we already own, and why it does not decide this

Look again at what Lesson 5.4 built, with §2.2's picture in mind:

The three arrays of engine::pool<T> mapped onto the three arrays of a sparse set.
engine::pool<T>a sparse setrole
slots_sparse indexed by key, one entry per key ever issued, stable
items_data the components, packed, no holes, order not guaranteed
owners_dense the way back: which key owns element d

That is not a resemblance, it is the same data structure. pool<T> is a sparse set with a generation counter welded to each sparse slot — which is exactly what an ECS wants, because an entity id has to survive the entity being destroyed and the slot being reused.

And it still does not settle the question, for two reasons worth being explicit about. The first is that pool<T> mints its own keys. A handle from the mesh pool is meaningless to the texture pool, because both hand out slot 0 to their first occupant. An ECS needs the opposite: one id space, minted once, with every pool keyed by it. That is a real change, not a reuse — §6 says exactly what it amounts to.

The second is the one that matters more. If owning a sparse set is allowed to decide that we should build a sparse-set ECS, then the architecture was chosen by an accident of what Lesson 5.4 happened to need for meshes. The archetype design would not use pool<T> at all, and "we would have to write more code" is a reason to prefer something, not a reason to believe it is faster. The convenience is admissible as a tiebreaker and inadmissible as evidence. So we measure first, and §6 is allowed to reach for it only after the numbers are in.

4The Experiment

4.1Simulating a design you have not built

The probe is one header, and the first thing to say about it is what is not in it. There is no entity manager, no component registry, no type erasure, no view type, no system scheduler, and no way to create an entity. Every one of those is required by a real ECS and none of them differs between the two designs, so including them would put a thousand lines of identical machinery between us and the two numbers we came for.

What is in it is the two access patterns. An archetype is four parallel std::vectors and a shared index; a sparse set is, per type, three vectors and a redirect. Both hold the same entities with the same component values, generated from the entity id by the same integer hash Lesson 5.6 used, so the two arms can be compared bit for bit.

Twenty lines, and everything the sparse-set design claims is visible in them: the insert is two appends and a write, the erase touches one pool, and neither has any way to know what else the entity owns. Compare that with the archetype's move, which needs a source chunk, a destination chunk, a per-entity archetype map, a per-entity row map, and a re-pack — a hundred lines in the same file, and it is not padded.

C++ in place — if constexpr, and why K is a template parameter

The query is written once and instantiated for K = 1, 2, 3, 4. Making K a template parameter rather than a runtime argument matters for a benchmark: if constexpr (K >= 2) is resolved at compile time, so the K = 2 instantiation contains no branch, no dead pointer and no unused load. A runtime if would leave a predictable branch in the loop, and predictable branches are cheap but not free — at 0.5 ns per item, "not free" is 10% of the measurement.

The same trick carries the two body variants as a bool Real. Two template parameters produce eight query functions from one piece of source, which is the property that matters here: every arm runs the same code, so a difference in the source cannot be a difference in the result.

4.2Being fair to the archetype

A benchmark comparing your preferred design against a strawman is a benchmark whose result you already know. Three deliberate concessions were made to the archetype, and each one costs it nothing that a real implementation would not also avoid.

Its columns are statically typed. The probe's chunk holds std::vector<engine::transform>, not a type-erased byte buffer with a per-archetype column index that has to be looked up. A real archetype ECS cannot do this — the set of component types is not known at compile time, so a query must resolve "which column is transform in this archetype" per archetype per query, and its inner loop indexes through a pointer it obtained at run time. The probe skips that cost entirely. Every archetype number below is therefore an upper bound on how good a real archetype ECS would be.

Its query really does skip non-matching entities. The chunk carries a columns count and the query walks only chunks with at least K of them, so in the selectivity test the archetype visits a quarter of the world and the other three quarters cost it literally nothing — not even a rejected test. That is the archetype's genuine structural advantage and the experiment gives it in full.

Its fragmented case is measured, not assumed. §5.4 splits the same entities across up to 4,096 archetypes and reports what that costs, rather than asserting that fragmentation is a disaster because the sparse-set literature says so.

4.3Being fair to the sparse set: the alignment axis

The sparse set gets one concession too, and it is the axis most published comparisons leave out. Ask the question directly: when the query reads velocity.sparse[e], where in memory does it land?

A pool's dense order is its insertion order. In a world built all at once — spawn ten thousand entities in a loop, each with the same four components — every pool receives the same entities in the same sequence, so every pool's dense order is identical. The query walks the transform pool in order, and velocity.sparse[e] therefore also walks in order. The redirect is a sequential read that the hardware prefetcher will have already fetched. It costs almost nothing.

Now run the game for ten minutes. Things spawn and die, components get added when a character picks up a torch and removed when it drops it, and every erase swaps some unrelated entity into the hole. The pools' orders drift apart, and eventually there is no relationship between position in one pool and position in another. The same source code is now performing random access into a 1.6 MB array.

Both are real, and a comparison that quotes one is not a comparison

The benchmark therefore builds the sparse world twice: once aligned (every pool receives entities in id order) and once scrambled (each secondary pool receives them in an independent random permutation). These are the best and worst case of the same design, they differ by up to 1.8× in the results below, and the truth for any given world is somewhere between them. The lead pool is in id order in both, so the two arms visit entities in the same sequence — which is what lets the harness demand bit-identical answers rather than a tolerance.

4.4Two bodies, because one cannot answer the question

§2.3 concluded that the redirect is latency and that latency can be hidden behind work. That is a hypothesis with an obvious test: run the same query with two different amounts of work in it and see whether the penalty changes.

The real body is deliberately the same workload Lesson 5.6 timed — build a model matrix with parent_from_local, then read all sixteen entries of it with layouts::sample. Reading all sixteen was 5.6's hard-won correction: reading five lets a compiler stop computing the other eleven in whichever arm it managed to inline, and 5.6 published a 3.4× that was really 1.7× before it caught that. Reusing the function rather than re-typing it means 5.7 cannot re-acquire the bug, and it puts both lessons' numbers on the same scale.

Everything else about the harness is Lesson 5.6's, unchanged: bench_compare alternates the arms one repetition each so drift lands on both, medians with minimum and maximum so a hiccup is visible, bench_keep so the loop is not deleted, a bias that differs per pass so the pass is not a pure function of nothing, and enough inner repetitions that a sample is worth ~100 µs against a 41.67 ns timer tick. Every ratio quoted below is taken within one alternating pairing, never between two absolutes from different pairings — 5.6 found the same arm reading 0.98 and 1.83 ns depending on who shared the cache with it.

5The Results

Everything below is from one machine — an Apple M4 Pro, 64 KB L1d, 4 MB L2 — built with Clang at -O2 -DNDEBUG, no LTO. Run it on yours; the shapes should survive and the exact sizes will move. python3 scratch/measure_57.py prints every table in this section.

5.1The query, and the control that says the harness works

Start with the control, because a benchmark you have not falsified is a benchmark you should not read. At K = 1 the query touches only the lead pool. Both designs are then doing literally the same thing — walking one dense array of transforms — so the ratio must be 1.00×, and any deviation would be the harness measuring itself rather than the designs. It is 0.99×–1.01× at every size on the real body. The instrument is honest.

Now K = 4, which is what a render query wants:

Sparse-set query cost relative to the archetype at K equals four, for two body costs and two pool alignments, across five world sizes.
K = 4, relative to archetype41001,000 10,000100,000
archetype, ns/item (real body)2.041.751.79 1.912.04
cheap body, aligned0.81×0.91×1.06× 1.26×1.31×
cheap body, scrambled0.84×0.98×1.16× 1.92×2.40×
real body, aligned0.97×1.05×1.05× 1.02×0.96×
real body, scrambled0.98×1.05×1.05× 1.17×1.34×

Three readings, in order of how much they matter.

Below a thousand entities, nothing. Every arm is within 16% of the archetype and most are within 5%. This is 5.6's finding arriving again by a different route: the working set is small enough that every access is a hit, and a redirect to somewhere already in L1 costs a few cycles that the core has nothing better to do with. If your world is a few hundred entities, this entire lesson's query argument is worth zero nanoseconds to you.

The scrambled and aligned arms differ by nearly a factor of two at 100,000. On the cheap body, 1.31× against 2.40×. Same code, same data, same design — the only difference is whether the pools happen to be in the same order. Any comparison that reports one number for "sparse set" is reporting whichever of these its benchmark happened to build.

The body dilutes everything. The worst case falls from 2.40× to 1.34× purely because the query does a matrix multiply per entity instead of one addition. That is the hypothesis from §2.3 confirmed, and §5.2 is the experiment that turns it from a plausible story into a mechanism.

5.2The redirect is latency, and latency hides

There is one loose end in the table above and it is at the far left: the cheap arms at n = 4 read 0.81× — the sparse set beating the archetype by 19% on four entities in L1, which no memory effect can explain. Worse, the full data has the archetype's own K = 3 column at n = 4 costing 2.58 ns against 0.87 ns at K = 4. A cost that is not monotonic in the amount of work is not a cost, it is a compiler.

Lesson 5.6's answer to this applies unchanged: rebuild with the vectoriser off and see what moves. One experiment, and it does two jobs.

The same K equals four query rebuilt with vectorisation disabled, for both bodies.
K = 4, -fno-vectorize -fno-slp-vectorize4 1001,00010,000100,000
archetype ns/item, cheap (was 0.87 at n=4)0.560.56 0.600.820.80
cheap body, aligned1.17×1.20×1.29× 1.37×1.49×
cheap body, scrambled1.15×1.20×1.26× 2.17×2.79×
real body, aligned0.99×0.99×0.98× 1.02×1.02×
real body, scrambled1.00×1.00× 1.01×1.21×1.39×

Job one: the n = 4 anomalies are gone. The archetype's non-monotonic K = 3 column collapses from 2.58 ns to 0.53, and the cheap arms stop beating the archetype at four entities. They were vectorisation decisions — the auto-vectoriser succeeding on one arm's four-iteration loop and not another's — which is a property of the compiler and of nothing else. This is why the left-hand columns of the vectorised table should be read as "no difference" rather than as numbers.

Job two, and it is the mechanism. Look at the real body with codegen held still: 1.00×, 1.00×, 1.01× up to a thousand entities, on the scrambled world. Not "close to". The redirect, performed with the pools in completely unrelated orders, costs nothing at all — and then costs 1.21× at ten thousand and 1.39× at a hundred thousand.

The finding, stated as a mechanism rather than a ratio

The sparse set's extra loads are latency in the shadow of work already being done. While the core is executing the nine multiplies and sixteen stores of parent_from_local for entity i, it has already issued the redirect loads for entities i+1 and beyond, because those addresses are available and independent. The loads finish before anyone asks for them.

Two things break the shadow, and both must happen together. The working set must exceed cache, so the latency to hide grows from ~4 cycles to ~200; and the pools must be out of order, so the prefetcher cannot have fetched the line already. Aligned at 100,000 is 1.02×; scrambled at 1,000 is 1.01×; scrambled at 100,000 is 1.39×. Neither condition alone costs anything.

That also tells you which systems in a real engine will and will not care. A render query building matrices, a physics integrator doing collision maths, a skinning pass blending matrices — these all have work to hide behind. A system that adds two floats across a hundred thousand entities does not, and that is exactly where the cheap body's 2.79× lives.

5.3The structural change, and the number that scales

Now the operation that points the other way. Each frame, 1% of the world gains a component and loses it again — an entity picking something up, a projectile being marked for despawn, a character entering a state. Measured per operation, at two entity widths.

Nanoseconds per component add or remove in each design, at two entity widths, across five world sizes.
ns per add or remove41001,000 10,000100,000
archetype, 4 components (108 B)15.3815.2512.25 15.0013.10
sparse set, 4 components9.569.424.50 5.314.25
ratio1.61×1.62× 2.72×2.82×3.08×
archetype, 12 components (236 B)29.6831.4240.92 38.9658.48
sparse set, 12 components10.0510.424.58 4.384.23
ratio2.95×3.02× 8.93×8.90×13.83×

The ratio is not the finding. The finding is which row moved. Widening the entity from four components to twelve — adding eight components that the operation does not read, does not write and has no logical relationship to — takes the archetype from 13.10 ns to 58.48 ns. The sparse set goes from 4.25 to 4.23, which is the same number.

That is §3.2's cost model confirmed and its correction confirmed with it. The archetype's move is two memory touches per column, and the columns are what widened; the sparse set's insert is three writes into one pool, and the pool did not widen because pools never do.

Four components is a number that flatters the archetype

The probe's entity has four. A shipping game entity has ten to thirty — a transform, a parent link, a mesh, a material, a bounding volume, a rigid body, a collider, a health value, a team, an AI state, a sound emitter, a light, an animation state, a network id. This course's entity will pass twelve during Module 7, when rigid bodies and skeletons arrive. The 13.83× column is the one to plan against, not the 3.08× one.

Both designs' small-world numbers deserve a note, because they look wrong. At n = 4 the sparse set costs 9.56 ns rather than 4.25 — more than at 100,000. That is not the world size, it is the churn set: 1% of four entities is one entity, so a "frame" is two operations and the per-frame overhead (building the set, the loop setup) is divided by two instead of by two thousand. It is measurement overhead, it applies equally to both arms, and the ratio in that column is still meaningful even though the absolute is not.

5.4Fragmentation, measured, and smaller than advertised

The standard argument against archetypes is fragmentation: every distinct component set is a separate chunk, so a game with lots of rare combinations ends up with thousands of tiny archetypes and the contiguity that justified the design evaporates. It is stated confidently and almost never measured. Here it is, at K = 4 on the real body:

Archetype query cost as the same entities are split across more archetypes, at two world sizes.
archetypes10,000 entities 100,000 entities
eachvs 1 chunkeachvs 1 chunk
81,2501.03×12,5000.99×
641561.05×1,5621.00×
512191.12×1951.06×
4,09621.07×241.08×

Twelve percent, worst case, with two entities per archetype. That is far less than the argument implies, and the reason is visible once stated: a chunk of two entities is still contiguous, still prefetched, and the per-chunk overhead is a loop setup, not a cache miss. Splitting a contiguous array into 4,096 contiguous arrays does not stop it being contiguous.

Be careful about what this does and does not license. It measures iteration over fragmented archetypes, which is the part the probe can simulate honestly. It does not measure what a real archetype ECS additionally pays as archetypes multiply: resolving which archetypes match a query (a set operation over thousands of them, usually cached but the cache has to be invalidated on every structural change), the per-archetype column lookup §4.2 gave the probe for free, and the allocator pressure of thousands of small chunks. Those are real and this lesson does not measure them — so the honest summary is that fragmentation's iteration cost is small, its bookkeeping cost is unmeasured here, and the case against archetypes should not lean on this argument.

5.5Selectivity, and the group that answers it

Now the archetype's best case, and the one the argument should be judged on. Give one entity in four a velocity and query {transform, velocity}. The archetype visits exactly the matching quarter, contiguously. The sparse set walks its dense velocity pool — also exactly the matching quarter — and reaches into a transform pool where only one element in four is wanted, so every cache line it fetches contains one useful transform and two useless ones.

Selective query cost relative to the archetype on the real body, for three sparse-set strategies plus a maintained group.
1 entity in 4 matches, real body41001,000 10,000100,000
archetype, ns per matching entity3.801.571.39 1.521.47
lead with the small pool0.50×1.16×1.19× 1.50×1.46×
…and with the pools scrambled0.49×1.13×1.18× 1.38×1.94×
lead with the big pool and skip0.62×1.16×1.21× 1.58×1.73×
grouped0.48×0.99×1.01× 1.01×0.99×

(The n = 4 column is one matching entity behind a two-chunk outer loop; it is loop overhead, not a result. Read from 100 rightwards.)

The first three rows are the archetype winning, properly, by 1.46× to 1.94× on a real body at scale — a bigger margin than the all-match query gave it, exactly as §2.1 predicted. Two of those rows are also an implementation lesson worth having: leading with the small pool rather than the big one is one line in a view's constructor, it means no entity is ever visited only to be rejected, and it is worth 1.73× → 1.46×. The "sparse sets are bad at selective queries because they have to skip" claim is about a naive implementation, not about the design.

And then the last row, which is why this lesson's recommendation is confident rather than grudging.

A group is an archetype you can add later

A pool's dense order is nobody's business but the pool's — no id outside it depends on where an element sits. So two pools can be sorted into a common order, putting the entities that have both components at the front of both dense arrays in the same sequence. Once that holds, index i of one array and index i of the other are the same entity, the query needs no sparse read at all, and what you are iterating is — byte for byte, access for access — an archetype chunk.

EnTT calls this a group. It is not a different storage design; it is a maintenance obligation laid on top of one, paid as a couple of swaps on insert and erase for the pools involved. Measured: 0.99× to 1.01× of the archetype at every size, on the archetype's best case.

So the archetype's query advantage is not a permanent property of the archetype. It is something a sparse set can buy, per query, when a measurement says that query needs it, at the price of slower structural changes for the two or three component types in that group — and at no cost to the other thirty.

6The Decision

Assemble it. On this machine, at the sizes measured, with both designs given their best implementation:

Summary of every measured difference between the two designs.
quantityarchetypesparse setat 100,000 entities
Query, all match, real bodybaseline0.96× – 1.34× archetype by up to 34%
Query, 1 in 4 match, real bodybaseline1.46× – 1.94× archetype by up to 94%
…the same, with a groupbaseline0.99× a tie
Query, cheap body, worst casebaseline2.40× archetype, substantially
Structural change, 4 components13.10 ns4.25 ns sparse by 3.1×
Structural change, 12 components58.48 ns4.23 ns sparse by 13.8×
Index memory, 4 types400 KB3.2 MBarchetype by 2.8 MB
Index memory, 32 types400 KB15.2 MBarchetype by 14.8 MB
Lines of code to implement~100 in the probe~20 in the probe sparse, by a lot

This course builds a sparse set. Four reasons, in the order of how much weight they carry.

1. The migration only runs one way. This is the argument, and everything else is supporting detail. A sparse set can be given an archetype's query performance later, incrementally, one group at a time, guided by a profile, without changing what a component is or invalidating a single id — measured at 0.99× on the archetype's own best case. An archetype cannot be given a sparse set's O(1) structural change at all, at any price, because moving between archetypes is what an archetype is. Choosing the design that can become the other one is not a preference; it is the option with strictly more future in it.

2. The cost we would be accepting is bounded and the cost we would be avoiding is not. The archetype's query win is at most 1.94×, only above ten thousand entities, only when a query is selective, and only on the arms we have not grouped. The archetype's structural-change loss is 13.8× and it grows with every component type this course adds — Module 6 adds materials, Module 7 adds rigid bodies, colliders and skeletal state. A cost that scales with the future of the codebase is worse than a larger cost that does not.

3. Nothing at this engine's scale is on the table anyway. Below a thousand entities every arm is within 16% and most within 5%. The demos have four objects. Module 8's capstone will have hundreds. We are choosing the design that will be right at 105 entities while paying nothing either way at 102, which means the decision can be made on the properties above rather than on today's frame time.

4. It is a quarter of the code, and this is a course. Twenty lines against a hundred in the probe, and the gap widens in a real implementation: an archetype needs type-erased columns, chunk allocation, an archetype graph with add/remove edges, and a query cache invalidated on every structural change. A student must be able to read the whole container. This reason is a tiebreaker, admissible only because points 1–3 already decided it — which is what §3.4 promised.

When this is the wrong answer — the one sentence

Choose the archetype when your world is large (past ~104 entities), its component sets are stable (structural changes are rare rather than per-frame), and your systems are narrow (little arithmetic per entity, so there is nothing for the redirect to hide behind) — a streaming open world, a crowd simulation, a particle or boid system with millions of near-identical agents. That is precisely the shape Unity DOTS is built for, and on that shape the 1.46×–1.94× becomes the dominant term while the 13.8× almost never fires.

And note what the escape hatch does to even that: if the engine ever reaches that shape, the fix is to group the two or three pools that matter, not to rewrite the storage.

Two consequences for what 5.8 builds, both of which follow from the measurements rather than from taste. Components must stay small and single-purpose, because the sparse set's whole model is that a system pays only for the pools it names — a fat component drags unread bytes through cache exactly as 5.6's 96-byte scene_object did. And the entity id must be one shared id space, which is the change §3.4 identified: pool<T>'s pattern is right, but the key must be minted by an entity allocator and handed to every pool, instead of each pool minting its own. 5.8 will lift 5.4's generational index out of pool<T> and make it the entity — which is the second time this course has been able to build a new system mostly out of a decision made two lessons earlier for a different reason.

7Complete Code Listings

Files created or modified in this lesson.

No engine source changes this lesson. Not one file under engine/ or demos/ is touched, the CMake build is unchanged, and the golden screenshot is byte-identical for the seventh lesson running. That is deliberate: the output of a design lesson is a decision, and a decision that quietly rewrote the renderer on its way past would be a different lesson.

8Build & Run

Everything here builds against the engine library the earlier lessons produced, so build that first. The commands are the same on all three platforms except for the shell.

⚠ VERIFY — the MSVC line is the shape, not a transcript

The Unix invocations above are what was actually run to produce every number in this lesson. The MSVC one follows the same pattern the earlier modules' harnesses use, but the exact library paths depend on your generator and configuration — check build\engine\ for where engine.lib landed. If in doubt, add scratch/verify_57.cpp as a target in CMakeLists.txt and let CMake work out the linkage, which is what a real project would do anyway.

What you should see. verify_57 prints seven sections and ends with 54 checks, 0 failures at both optimisation levels. measure_57.py prints six numbered sections; the shapes to look for are the ones §5 discusses — a K = 1 row that is 1.00× (your instrument is honest), a left half where everything is within a few percent of 1.00× (your world is small), a scrambled-versus-aligned gap that opens somewhere between 1,000 and 100,000 (your cache), a structural-change row that does not move when the entity widens (the sparse set), and one that quadruples (the archetype).

Your knee will not be at the same size as this machine's, and that is the point of running it rather than reading it. Divide your L2 size by 108 bytes and see whether it lands where your numbers turn.

9Common Pitfalls & Debugging

The row map written before the re-pack

Symptom: an archetype ECS that is correct for thousands of moves and then silently corrupts one entity. Cause: add_material originally recorded where the entity landed before calling pop_row on the source chunk. pop_row moves the source's last row into the hole and patches that entity's row index — and when the moved entity is the last row, that entity is the one being moved, so the stale index overwrites the fresh one.

Fix: re-pack first, then record. How it was found: a 1-in-N event needs N tries, so verify_57 §E churns 200 frames and then walks the entire row map. A single-frame test would have passed for weeks.

A benchmark whose arms visit entities in different orders

Symptom: bench_ab::agree is false and you reach for a tolerance. Cause: usually the arms sum the same numbers in a different sequence, which floating-point addition does not promise to give the same answer for — 5.6 hit exactly this. But: reaching for a tolerance disables the check that would catch an arm which is fast because it is doing less.

Fix here: make the orders the same by construction. All three arms walk the lead pool in entity-id order, so every ratio in this lesson is between arms that agree to the last bit. Prefer arranging for exactness over tolerating inexactness — and when you genuinely cannot, report the magnitude, as bench_ab::max_rel_diff does.

Comparing "sparse set" against "archetype" with one number

Symptom: your benchmark says 1.05× and someone else's says 2.4×, and you conclude one of you is wrong. Cause: you built the pools in the same order and they did not, or your body did real work and theirs did not. Those two axes span a range of 1.00× to 2.79× in this lesson's own data, for identical source code.

Fix: state the alignment and the per-entity work with every number you publish, and measure both ends of each. A comparison with one number for a two-parameter family is not a comparison.

Reading the small-n columns as results

Symptom: a table where cost is not monotonic in the amount of work — here, the archetype's cheap query costing 2.58 ns at K = 3 and 0.87 ns at K = 4, on four entities. Cause: at four entities the loop is four iterations and the auto-vectoriser's decisions dominate everything else.

Fix: rebuild with -fno-vectorize -fno-slp-vectorize. If the anomaly disappears — it does, 2.58 → 0.53 — it was codegen. Do not explain a compiler with a cache.

Leading a selective view with the largest pool

Symptom: a sparse-set query over {transform, rare_thing} that costs the same whether one entity in four matches or one in a thousand. Cause: the view is walking the transform pool and testing each entity for rare_thing, so it visits the whole world to find a handful.

Fix: walk the smallest pool. It is one comparison in the view's constructor and it is worth 1.73× → 1.46× at one-in-four; the further the selectivity goes, the more it is worth. 5.8's view must do this, and it is the reason a pool has to be able to report its own size cheaply.

Treating a sparse array as free because it is only indices

Symptom: memory use that grows when you add a component type nothing uses yet. Cause: a sparse array is sized by the entity id space, not by the number of components — so a type with three instances in a 100,000-entity world still costs 400 KB. Fix: keep the id space compact (5.4's free list already does), do not make short-lived swarms into entities, and if a real world needs 32+ types at 105 entities, page the sparse arrays the way EnTT does.

10Exercises

  1. 10.1 — Find the crossover on your machine. §6 argues from a rate comparison it never actually performs. Do it: using your own numbers, solve for the number of structural changes per frame at which the sparse set's total cost equals the archetype's, for a world of 10,000 entities running one K = 4 query per frame. Then do it again for a 12-component entity.

    Hint

    Query cost is N × (ratio − 1) × archetype-ns; structural cost is C × (archetype-ns − sparse-ns). Set them equal and solve for C.

    Solution sketch

    With this machine's numbers — 10,000 entities, real body, scrambled (1.17×, 1.91 ns archetype) — the sparse set gives up 10,000 × 0.17 × 1.91 ≈ 3,250 ns of query per frame, and wins 15.00 − 5.31 = 9.7 ns per structural change. It breaks even at ≈ 335 structural changes per frame, or 3.4% of the world. At twelve components it is 34.6 ns per change and break-even falls to ≈ 94. Two things are worth noticing: the number is small enough that a game with any real spawning reaches it, and it is a per-frame budget you can actually go and count in your own game.

  2. 10.2 — Page the sparse array. §3.3 argues this course does not need paging because 5.4's free list keeps ids compact. Test the cost of being wrong: change pool<T> in the probe to store sparse as a vector of 4,096-entry pages, and re-run the query benchmark.

    Hint

    sparse[e] becomes pages[e >> 12][e & 4095]. That is one more dependent load, so the chain in Figure 2 goes from depth 2 to depth 3.

    Solution sketch

    Expect near-zero cost on the aligned world and on the real body below the knee — the page pointer is one line that stays hot and the extra latency hides. Expect it to be visible on the scrambled cheap body at 100,000, where there was nothing to hide behind already. The interesting part is that it costs memory-bandwidth nothing: the page table is 4 bytes per 4,096 entities. Paging trades a small latency you can usually hide for a large footprint you cannot.

  3. 10.3 — Make the group pay for itself. §5.5 measures what a group is worth on a query but never measures what it costs to maintain. Add a fourth structural arm: a sparse set that keeps {transform, velocity} grouped, so every insert and erase of either component must swap the affected element across the group boundary. Measure it against the ungrouped sparse set.

    Hint

    Maintaining a group is: on insert, if the entity now has both, swap it to position group_size in both pools and increment. On erase, swap it out and decrement. Each swap is two element moves plus two sparse patches, per pool.

    Solution sketch

    You should find it costs on the order of a few nanoseconds per structural change on the grouped types only — a fraction of the archetype's, because it moves two components rather than all of them. That is the whole shape of the argument in §6 reduced to one measurement: a group buys the archetype's query at the archetype's price, but charges it only on the pools you chose, instead of on every component the entity happens to own.

  4. 10.4 — Type-erase the archetype's columns. §4.2 admits the probe's archetype is faster than a real one because its columns are statically typed. Quantify the concession: store each column as a std::vector<std::byte> plus an element size, look up the column index per archetype at query time, and re-run §5.1.

    Hint

    The inner loop's addressing becomes base + i * stride with a stride the compiler cannot see, which blocks some vectorisation. Take the scalar build too, so you can tell the codegen cost from the memory cost — the move this lesson made twice.

    Solution sketch

    Expect the loss to be modest and real, and concentrated on the cheap body where the loop was tight enough for vectorisation to matter. However large it turns out on your machine, subtract it from every archetype-favouring number in §5 — which is what "upper bound" in §4.2 means, and which strengthens §6 rather than weakening it.

  5. 10.5 — Argue the other side. Write the design document that chooses the archetype, using only the numbers in this lesson, and make it honest — no straw men, no numbers omitted. Then write down what it would take to change this course's mind.

    Hint

    The strongest archetype case is not the query ratio. It is the memory table plus a claim about what the engine will look like at Module 8.

    Solution sketch

    The best version runs: at 32 component types the sparse set pays 14.8 MB of index that the archetype does not, which is 3.7× this machine's L2 and is therefore not merely footprint but a permanent tax on every cache the engine uses; the query win is 1.46×–1.94× on exactly the selective queries a mature engine has most of; and the structural-change loss can be mitigated by deferring changes to a command buffer and applying them in bulk at a frame boundary, which is what Unity DOTS does and which the 13.83× column does not model. That last point is the real one, and the honest answer to "what would change our mind" is: a measurement of batched structural change. If moving a thousand entities between two archetypes at once costs much less than a thousand individual moves — and it should, since the destination appends become one memcpy — then the archetype's worst column is an artefact of measuring the operation one at a time. That experiment is not in this lesson, it is the strongest thing that could be, and it is exercise 10.5 rather than a table because it needs a batching API neither probe has.

11Recap & Next

The engine gained no code this lesson and gained the thing it most needed: a decided architecture with its evidence attached. When 5.8's container turns out to be awkward somewhere — and it will — the question "should this have been an archetype?" now has an answer with numbers behind it, and a stated condition under which the answer changes.

What was established:

  • Both designs satisfy every constraint 5.6 produced, so 5.6's numbers genuinely could not decide this, and a new measurement was necessary rather than rhetorical.
  • A design can be measured before it is built, when the difference between the candidates is an access pattern. Three hundred lines answered a question that would otherwise have cost two implementations and a sunk-cost argument.
  • The sparse set's redirect is latency, and latency hides. With codegen held still it costs nothing — 1.00× — until the working set leaves cache and the pools' orders have diverged. Both conditions are needed; either alone is free.
  • The archetype's structural change carries the entity's width in it and the sparse set's does not: 13.10 → 58.48 ns against 4.25 → 4.23 ns when the entity goes from four components to twelve.
  • A group is an archetype you can add later, measured at 0.99× of a real archetype on the archetype's own best case — which makes the migration one-directional and is the reason for the recommendation.
  • Fragmentation's iteration cost is 12% at worst, far less than the argument against archetypes implies, and this lesson says so even though it argues the other way.

Next: 5.8 — The ECS Runtime. It builds what this lesson chose. The entity becomes a generational id in one shared space — 5.4's handle, lifted out of pool<T> and given its own allocator — a component pool becomes a sparse set keyed by it, and a view becomes the thing that walks the smallest pool and looks the rest up, because §5.5 measured what leading with the wrong one costs. Then, and only then, the engine gets its first system, and the demo scene stops being four scene_objects in a std::vector.

12Further Reading

  • Michele Caini (skypjack), “ECS back and forth”. The series behind EnTT, and the best available written account of sparse sets as an ECS storage — including groups, which parts 4 and 5 develop. Read it after this lesson and you will recognise every structure.
  • The EnTT source, entt/entity/sparse_set.hpp and storage.hpp. Denser than the articles and worth the effort: the paged sparse array, the version bits inside the entity, and the group machinery are all there in a form you can now read.
  • Unity's Entities documentation on chunks and archetypes. The clearest first-party description of the archetype model, including the 16 KB chunk size and change filtering. Read it as the strongest case for the side this lesson did not take.
  • Sander Mertens, the Flecs manual and his “Building an ECS” posts. An archetype implementation whose author writes candidly about the costs — particularly the archetype graph and what it does to structural changes.
  • Adam Martin, “Entity Systems are the future of MMOG development” (2007). The historical starting point. Worth reading for how much of the modern argument is already there, and how much of it was about editors and iteration speed rather than about caches.
  • Bjarne Stroustrup / Chandler Carruth on data structure choice. Whenever a "which container" argument stalls, the answer is usually the one this lesson took: measure the two operations that differ, and prefer the option that can become the other one.
  • Lesson 5.6 of this course. Not padding: §5.2's latency-hiding result only makes sense against 5.6's memory-level-parallelism measurement, and the vectoriser experiment in §5.2 is 5.6's method applied unchanged.

version: 1.0
updated: 2026-09-04 (after Lesson 5.7 — 52 of 94 lessons)

conventions:
  ecs-storage: THE ECS IS A SPARSE SET, DECIDED IN 5.7 BY MEASUREMENT, NOT TASTE.
        One dense array per component TYPE plus a sparse map entity -> dense index.
        NOT an archetype. The argument is NOT "sparse sets are faster" — on the
        query the archetype wins — it is that THE MIGRATION ONLY RUNS ONE WAY:
        a sparse set can be given an archetype's query later, one GROUP at a time
        (measured 0.99x of a real archetype on the archetype's own best case),
        and an archetype can never be given O(1) structural change, because
        moving between archetypes IS what an archetype is.
        THE FOUR RULES THAT FOLLOW, and 5.8 must honour all four:
          1 COMPONENTS STAY SMALL AND SINGLE-PURPOSE. The whole model is that a
            system pays only for the pools it names; a fat component drags unread
            bytes exactly as 5.6's 96-byte scene_object did.
          2 ONE SHARED ID SPACE. pool<T>'s three arrays are already a sparse set
            (slots_=sparse, items_=data, owners_=dense) but each pool MINTS ITS
            OWN keys, so a mesh handle means nothing to a texture pool. The
            entity id must be minted once and handed to every pool.
          3 A VIEW LEADS WITH THE SMALLEST POOL. One comparison in the view's
            constructor, worth 1.73x -> 1.46x at one-in-four selectivity, and
            worth more the rarer the component. Never walk the big pool and skip.
          4 NO PAGED SPARSE ARRAYS YET, and the reason is 5.4: the free list
            reuses the most recently freed slot, so the id space's high-water
            mark is peak LIVE entities, not entities ever created. 10 types x
            10k peak = 400 KB. Revisit at 32 types x 1e5 (12.8 MB, 3x L2).
  measurement: A/B TIMING HAS FOUR RULES AND engine/core/bench.hpp IS THEM.
        1 ALTERNATE THE ARMS — one rep of A, one of B, microseconds apart, never
          in blocks. 3.10 published a 10% "improvement" that was session drift.
        2 REPORT THE MEDIAN AND THE SPREAD. A hiccup moves a mean, not a median;
          a median alone hides a bimodal result. If one arm's range straddles the
          other's median the honest word is "tie".
        3 STOP THE OPTIMISER DELETING THE WORK. bench_keep() stores through an
          `inline volatile double`, which the standard requires to happen.
        4 CHECK BOTH ARMS COMPUTED THE SAME ANSWER. bench_ab carries `agree`
          (bit-identical) AND `max_rel_diff`, because an arm that VISITS THE SAME
          ELEMENTS IN A DIFFERENT ORDER sums them in a different order and FP
          addition is not associative. Exact is the right default; >1e-9 is not
          rounding, it is different work.
        QUOTE RATIOS WITHIN A PAIRING, NEVER ABSOLUTES ACROSS PAIRINGS. At large N
        the two arms evict each other, so one arm's own median moved 0.98 -> 1.83
        ns/item depending on who it was measured against. Alternation protects
        against thermal drift and INTRODUCES cache interference; both arms in a
        pairing paid the same interference.
        THREE WAYS A MICROBENCHMARK LIES, all three hit in 5.6:
          - the timer is coarser than the work (24 MHz => 41.67 ns per tick; a
            pass over 4 objects read 0 or 1 ticks). Tell: a round number, often 0.
          - the compiler deleted the loop (a pure function of nothing, repeated).
            Tell: A NUMBER THAT IS NOT PHYSICALLY POSSIBLE — 0.166 ns for four
            matrix builds is 2.3 cycles for 9 multiplies and 16 stores.
          - the accumulator IS the bottleneck (`hits += 1.0` is a ~4-cycle serial
            chain). Tell: two arms that should differ, agreeing exactly.
        SANITY-CHECK EVERY PUBLISHED NUMBER AGAINST THE HARDWARE. ns -> cycles ->
        instructions, and instructions are countable. No tool finds this one.
        AN EFFECT THAT SURVIVES THE REMOVAL OF ITS EXPLANATION HAD A DIFFERENT
        EXPLANATION. Find the knob that turns off the hypothesised mechanism
        (-fno-vectorize; a scene small enough for L1; a monomorphic version of a
        polymorphic call) and see whether the effect goes with it.
        RELEASE BUILDS ONLY for any timing claim. A debug build measures the
        optimiser's absence.
  assets: AN ASSET IS FOUND BY NAME, LOADED ONCE, AND EXPLICITLY UNLOADED.
        A NAME IS NOT A PATH. "torus.obj" is stable, recorded in a scene file, and
        the key the store caches on; /Users/…/assets/torus.obj is where that name
        resolved TODAY. Store the first, compute the second.
        engine::search_path is an ORDERED LIST OF ROOTS, and order IS the feature:
        prepend_root shadows a shipped asset without moving or deleting anything —
        mods, localisation packs, live editing out of the source tree, and a test
        fixture standing in for a real asset are all the same mechanism.
        search_path::beside_executable(subdir) is THE ONLY CALLER OF
        SDL_GetBasePath() IN THE ENGINE (was 2 before 5.5, was 3 counting the
        demo's own path assembly). Assets, shaders and Module 7's sounds each get
        their own root from one rule.
        resolve() REFUSES a name that is absolute, empty, or contains "..", before
        any root is tried — SYNTACTICALLY, not by canonicalising, because
        canonicalisation needs the filesystem and grows bypasses. It also requires
        SDL_PATHTYPE_FILE: a directory of the right name is not a hit.
        NO REFERENCE COUNTING ON HANDLES, and the argument is from 5.4's
        properties rather than from taste: a refcount needs a copy constructor, a
        destructor and a pointer to the store, which costs 4 bytes/trivially
        copyable/memcpy-able-into-a-component/serializable-without-fixups — every
        property that made a handle worth having. A refcounted handle is a
        shared_ptr with extra steps.
        SO: EXPLICIT UNLOAD, safe to get wrong because 5.4 made staleness
        detectable. Forget to unload -> a LEAK, which live_count() finds. Unload
        too early -> a NULL and collect_stats::unresolved. Neither is a crash.
        DERIVED ASSETS ARE THE ONE PLACE A LIFETIME RULE IS UNAVOIDABLE: a derived
        asset is OWNED BY ITS SOURCE and unloading a source cascades TRANSITIVELY.
        It has no name, because nobody asked for it by one. Deriving from a stale
        source is REFUSED (an orphan has no name to find it by and no source to
        free it with). A cascade that stops after one hop is a leak.
        IMPORT SETTINGS ARE PART OF AN ASSET'S IDENTITY. The same file imported two
        ways is two meshes with two vertex arrays, so the key is name + settings
        (engine::mesh_import). THE DEFAULT CONFIGURATION MUST SERIALISE TO NOTHING:
        asset_key("torus.obj", {}) == "torus.obj", and only a non-default import
        earns a "|flip=0" suffix — otherwise generated content (bare name) and
        loaded content (decorated name) live in two key spaces wearing one name.
        THE NAME MAP IS A HINT; THE POOL IS THE TRUTH. Every cache probe nests a
        pool::contains() inside the map lookup, so an asset freed by handle leaves
        no live-looking entry. A pointer-keyed cache could never ask.
        LOAD FAILURE IS THE ORDINARY CASE: null handle + a report, logged ONCE at
        the point that knows why (resolved_path::refused exists so the caller can
        tell "already reported" from "mine to report"). No exception, no assertion,
        and NO FALLBACK ASSET — substituting a default cube removes the game's
        ability to notice.
        NOT A SINGLETON, and the reason is concrete rather than stylistic: sandbox
        holds THREE asset_stores in one program. platform and app deliberately do
        not own one either — that would be a singleton with better manners.
  handles: A REFERENCE INTO THE ENGINE IS AN INDEX PLUS A GENERATION, NEVER A
        POINTER. engine::handle<T> is ONE 32-BIT WORD split 20 index / 12
        generation (the same split EnTT uses for entt::entity), packed
        generation-high. T is a PHANTOM parameter — nothing of it is stored,
        sizeof is 4 for every T, T may be incomplete — so handle<mesh_data> and
        handle<texture> are DIFFERENT TYPES and a mix-up is a compile error.
        GENERATION 0 IS RESERVED, which makes the null handle all-bits-zero for
        free: a value-initialised member, a default-constructed vector element and
        a memset struct are all null with no code.
        THE THREE FAILURES, and this is the derivation, not a list:
          dangling    the object was freed
          aliasing    …and something took its place (ABA). A BARE INDEX MAKES
                      THIS WORSE — it converts a probable crash into a guaranteed
                      silent wrong answer, which is why the generation exists.
          relocation  nothing was freed; the container moved. An index survives
                      this and a pointer cannot.
        THE GENERATION IS BUMPED ON REMOVAL, never on insertion. A freed slot then
        already carries a value no outstanding handle has, so "is this slot
        occupied" needs no separate flag and no window exists in which a stale
        handle matches.
        THE BUDGET IS MONITORED, NOT ASSUMED. 4,095 usable generations means a
        slot's generation REPEATS after 4,095 frees of that slot — 68 s at 60 Hz,
        4.7 days at one level load per 100 s. Unreachable for assets, worth
        thinking about for entities. pool::generation_wraps() counts it and the
        pool logs a warning; if it ever leaves zero the two fixes are a FIFO free
        list (spreads reuse across all slots, multiplying time-to-wrap by the slot
        count) or 64 bits split 32/32.
        THE ONE RULE FOR USERS: RESOLVE LATE, USE IMMEDIATELY, NEVER STORE. get()
        returns a T* valid until the next insert or remove and not one instruction
        longer. Storing one re-creates the bug the design abolished, wearing
        modern clothes.
        RESOLVE AT THE BOUNDARY, ONCE PER OBJECT — not once per use and never once
        per vertex. Measured: 0.98 ns vs 0.85 ns for a raw dereference, so +0.13 ns
        per resolution; per object per frame that is 0.003% of a 60 Hz budget on a
        4,096-object scene, and per vertex it would be a different conversation.
        A HANDLE IS HALF A REFERENCE; THE POOL IS THE OTHER HALF. Every function
        that resolves one takes the pool as a parameter (collect_triangles went
        8 -> 9 params, and Lesson 5.1's reduction is not being walked back — this
        is the cost of the design, made visible rather than hidden in a global).
        A GLOBAL WAS REFUSED and there is a concrete reason: sandbox has TWO
        mesh_pools in one frame by the end of 5.4.
  logging: TWO AXES, NEVER ONE. A CATEGORY says WHO is speaking (a noun, a part of
        the program); a PRIORITY says HOW MUCH IT MATTERS. Collapsing them — a
        `LOG_ERROR` *category* — destroys the mechanism, because you can then never
        say "errors from the GPU but not from assets".
        FIVE CATEGORIES, based at SDL_LOG_CATEGORY_CUSTOM (never at a literal —
        SDL reserves everything above that enumerator for applications, and a
        number would collide the week SDL adds one):
          log_core  log_platform  log_gfx  log_gpu  log_asset
        A static_assert ties the name table to the enum, so adding one without a
        name does not compile. log_gfx currently has ZERO users: the CPU
        rasterizer never logs. Recorded rather than hidden; Module 6 fills it.
        SIX LEVELS, and the meanings are PROMISES that 92 call sites obey:
          error     the operation did not happen, and the caller is being told so
          warn      something went wrong and WE RECOVERED  <- the whole distinction
          info      a fact worth having on a bug report
          debug     what a subsystem did, once per operation
          trace     per-frame or per-item detail
          critical  the program cannot continue
        THE MECHANICAL TEST between error and warn: DID THE OPERATION HAPPEN?
        WHO LOGS A FAILURE: the DEEPEST point that knows WHY. Callers propagate
        SILENTLY and add only the CONSEQUENCE — the thing they know that the
        callee does not. Chosen over "the caller decides the level" because the
        cost of THIS rule is visible (a recovering caller gets an error line it
        did not deserve, fixable by demoting that call to debug) and the cost of
        the other is invisible (detail lost at every layer boundary).
        THE PAYOFF IS FREE AND THAT IS THE ARGUMENT AGAINST A WRAPPER. SDL's
        documented default table is `app=info,assert=warn,test=verbose,*=error`,
        so every category SDL does not know about — i.e. every one of ours —
        defaults to ERROR. Moving the engine off SDL_LOG_CATEGORY_APPLICATION
        makes it quiet with no configuration and no filtering code. A hand-rolled
        logger would have had to reimplement that and would have got a different
        default.
        DEMOS KEEP SDL_Log ON PURPOSE (122 of them). A demo IS the application;
        APPLICATION/info is exactly what its output is, and it is on by default
        because the person who ran the program asked for it.
        `--log SPEC` works on EVERY program: platform::start reads it first thing,
        and app_runner::init fills in argv when the app left it null. Grammar
        borrowed from SDL's own SDL_LOGGING hint so two things a person might type
        do not need two mental models. `*` means OUR categories only — silencing
        SDL's diagnostics is not ours to do on the user's behalf.
        THE PARSER VALIDATES EVERYTHING BEFORE APPLYING ANYTHING. A rejected spec
        is a NO-OP. Parsing straight into SDL_SetLogPriority would leave the good
        first entry applied after a bad third one, and the user would have a
        configuration they did not ask for and cannot see.
        THE FILE SINK CHAINS rather than replaces: SDL_GetLogOutputFunction before
        SDL_SetLogOutputFunction, and call the previous one. "Also log to a file"
        must not silently cost you your console. SDL holds a mutex across the
        hook, so it is thread-safe for free. SDL FILTERS BEFORE THE HOOK, so a
        message below its level never reaches the sink — the file and the level
        are independent controls.
  assertions: AN ASSERTION IS NOT AN ERROR, and one question separates them:
        COULD A CORRECT PROGRAM, ON A WORKING MACHINE, ENCOUNTER THIS?
          yes -> an ERROR. The world did it to you. Return it; it ships.
          no  -> an ASSERTION. Your own code is wrong. Stop; it may be compiled out.
        THREE MACROS:
          ENGINE_ASSERT(c)  debug only. The default. NO SIDE EFFECTS in c.
          ENGINE_CHECK(c)   every build. Only where continuing is worse than stopping.
          ENGINE_VERIFY(e)  the expression ALWAYS runs; the check is debug only.
        SDL DECIDES ITS LEVEL FROM __OPTIMIZE__, NOT NDEBUG (SDL_assert.h). So -O2
        ALONE takes you to SDL_ASSERT_LEVEL 1 — SDL_assert disabled,
        SDL_assert_release live — with no -DNDEBUG anywhere. OUR log floor keys off
        NDEBUG. TWO GATES, TWO SWITCHES: `-O2` gives dead assertions + live trace
        logging; `-O0 -DNDEBUG` gives the exact inverse. Neither is what you guess.
        SDL_disabled_assert WRAPS THE CONDITION IN sizeof — compiled, never
        evaluated. Good (the condition cannot rot, and `assert(fp = fopen(...))`
        still does not open the file) and a trap (this is why ENGINE_VERIFY exists).
        SDL_enabled_assert IS A `while (!(condition))` LOOP, so SDL_ASSERTION_RETRY
        genuinely RE-TESTS: fix state in a debugger, continue, proceed as though the
        bug had not happened. ALWAYS_IGNORE LATCHES in a static per expansion site.
        ASSERTIONS ARE TESTABLE: SDL_SetAssertionHandler returning IGNORE, plus
        SDL_GetAssertionReport()'s linked list (condition text, file, line,
        trigger_count). verify_53 §F proves an assertion fires on the input that
        should fire it — the thing everyone assumes is impossible.
  errors: THE ENGINE HAD ALREADY CONVERGED ON THE ANSWER TWICE. obj_report (3.5)
        and gpu_report (4.2) are both: a STATUS enum naming the failure precisely,
        the FACTS you ask for next, and a `bool ok()`. Written a module apart by
        nobody trying to match the other. 5.3 NAMES that shape rather than
        inventing a fourth, and converts the odd one out (image_status ->
        image_report).
        THE FOUR RULES:
          1. status + report + ok() when a caller might BRANCH on the failure, or
             when there are diagnostics worth having on SUCCESS too.
          2. A BARE `bool` IS HONEST WHEN THERE IS NOTHING MORE TO SAY
             (platform::set_vsync: one failure mode, one sane response).
          3. PER-SUBSYSTEM status enums, NOT one engine-wide one. A global enum
             becomes a junk drawer where bad_format means six things. The SHAPE is
             unified; the vocabularies are not, and should not be.
          4. "returns false and has ALREADY logged" is retired as an unwritten
             contract — it is now the documented who-logs rule above.
        A REPORT DESCRIBES WHAT IT REFUSED: image_report fills in width/height
        BEFORE the size check, so `too_large` says how large. A report that
        describes the thing it rejected is a diagnosis; one that does not is a
        complaint.
        std::expected IS C++23 AND WE ARE NOT ADOPTING IT YET. Reason recorded:
        the engine's loaders return FACTS, not just values, and they return them
        whether or not they worked — expected has nowhere to put obj_report's
        twelve statistics. Exercise 9.5 builds one anyway. ADOPT A NEW ABSTRACTION
        WHEN THE OLD ONE HAS FAILED YOU, NOT WHEN THE NEW ONE IS ELEGANT.
  program-entry: A PROGRAM PICKS ONE OF TWO ARRANGEMENTS AND THE ENGINE SUPPORTS
        BOTH. Library: construct engine::platform, write your own main() and your
        own while(plat.running()). Framework: derive from engine::app, override
        the hooks, and write ENGINE_MAIN(YourApp) — no main(), no loop.
        engine::app IS IMPLEMENTED ON engine::platform, NEVER BESIDE IT. Anything
        app can do must be reachable from the library path, or the library path
        has quietly become second-class. verify_52 §E renders six frames down each
        path and compares the framebuffers byte for byte.
        <engine/platform/main.hpp> GOES IN EXACTLY ONE .cpp PER PROGRAM, AND THAT
        FILE MUST NOT DEFINE main(). It defines SDL_MAIN_USE_CALLBACKS and
        includes <SDL3/SDL_main.h>, which emits a NON-INLINE definition of
        SDL_main() plus the platform entry point. Two inclusions = duplicate
        symbol _main. That is the ODR, not an SDL quirk — and it is why the entry
        point cannot live in libengine.a: an entry point is not a library's to own.
        It is DELIBERATELY ABSENT from engine.hpp, the umbrella: "include
        everything, it is harmless" must not be a way to acquire a main().
        THE FOUR SDL3 SIGNATURES, verified against SDL 3.4.12:
          SDL_AppResult SDL_AppInit   (void **appstate, int argc, char *argv[]);
          SDL_AppResult SDL_AppIterate(void *appstate);
          SDL_AppResult SDL_AppEvent  (void *appstate, SDL_Event *event);
          void          SDL_AppQuit   (void *appstate, SDL_AppResult result);
        Returns: SDL_APP_CONTINUE / SDL_APP_SUCCESS / SDL_APP_FAILURE.
  surfaces: THE SURFACE IS CHOSEN BEFORE ANYTHING EXISTS, in app_config, because
        by the time you could regret it the window already belongs to somebody.
          surface::renderer  SDL_Renderer + streaming texture (Module 1's path)
          surface::gpu       a window claimed by NOBODY, for gpu_device::create
          surface::headless  no window, and NO SDL_INIT_VIDEO — runs with no display
        This is Lesson 4.2's "a window is claimed by an SDL_GPU device or driven by
        an SDL_Renderer, never both" turned from a comment into an enum.
        HEADLESS DOES NOT INITIALISE VIDEO, and that is the point rather than an
        optimisation: SDL_Init(SDL_INIT_VIDEO) FAILS on a build server. The
        pre-5.2 `sandbox --shot` called it and never used it, so the
        characterization test built specifically for automation could not run
        anywhere automatic. verify_52 §B pins it (SDL_WasInit == 0).
  edges-and-levels: EDGES BELONG TO THE FRAME, LEVELS BELONG TO THE STEP.
        on_fixed_step runs 0..N times per frame (measured: 0,1,2,1,2 across five
        frames within 1.6 ms of each other at 60 Hz), so an edge query inside it
        fires TWICE on a two-step frame. key_down() in a step is safe — input is
        frame-coherent since Lesson 1.2, so every step in one frame sees the same
        snapshot. key_pressed() is not. Discrete presses go in on_event, where
        event.key.repeat is also available.
  resource-names: NAME EVERY GPU RESOURCE AT CREATION, through
        SDL_PROP_GPU_{BUFFER,TEXTURE,TRANSFERBUFFER}_CREATE_NAME_STRING — never
        through SDL_SetGPU{Buffer,Texture}Name. SDL's own docs on the setter:
        "You should use SDL_PROP_GPU_BUFFER_CREATE_NAME_STRING with
        SDL_CreateGPUBuffer instead of this function to avoid thread safety
        issues", and "This function is not thread safe".
        LESSON 4.3's COMMENT ABOUT THIS WAS WRONG AND IS NOW CORRECTED IN PLACE.
        It claimed the creation property existed for shaders "because a shader is
        immutable the moment it exists", inferring a reason from the asymmetry
        with the buffer/texture setters. There was no asymmetry to explain: the
        property is the recommended path for all three and the setters are the
        older API. A CONFIDENT EXPLANATION OF WHY SOMEBODY ELSE'S API IS SHAPED
        THE WAY IT IS, IS A HYPOTHESIS — and the cheapest place to test it is the
        documentation of the function you are already calling.
        NEITHER API HAS A GETTER. A name is write-only from the program's side, so
        it cannot be asserted on in a harness; only a capture shows it.
        engine::create_named_{buffer,texture,transfer_buffer} in gfx/gpu_debug.hpp
        are the wrappers. A failed property allocation costs the NAME and never
        the RESOURCE — a debugging aid must not be able to break what it aids.
        Cost: 0.0011 -> 0.0018 ms per buffer, paid once, at load.
  debug-groups: A DEBUG GROUP IS A C++ SCOPE, engine::debug_group, and it is
        NEITHER COPYABLE NOR MOVABLE. That is SDL's Metal rule expressed in the
        type system: "On some backends (e.g. Metal), pushing a debug group during
        a render/blit/compute pass will create a group that is scoped to the
        native pass rather than the command buffer. For best results, if you push
        a debug group during a pass, always pop it in the same pass."
        The engine pushes FOUR a frame (upload / clear / blit / scene) and opens
